Right. \n\nUm... well, besides the presentations, I've also been missing all this award-related networking stuff because I just can't face being seen by anyone in the industry. It's too humiliating. So my project deadlines are starting to completely fall apart because I'm just hiding out. And my family... I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister just had a big barbecue for my niece's graduation, and they catered from that place on 5th street with the amazing brisket. They smoke it over hickory for like fourteen hours, the bark on it is just incredible. I used to go there every Tuesday for lunch before they got super popular and raised their prices. \n\nBut anyway, I just... I couldn't go to the party. I stayed home. I just can't let people look at me right now, not even my own family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and mouth could be fixed or improved in some way, or do you see them as inherently flawed?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I'm not sure if it can actually be fixed, or if the underlying structure is just... wrong. Like it's just built wrong. \n\nIt's funny, when you use the phrase \"inherently flawed,\" it makes me think of this structural steel we got delivered for a cantilevered deck last spring. The mill actually sent us beams with these tiny micro-fractures in the flanges. You couldn't even see them with the naked eye, but the inspector caught it on the ultrasound test. If we had installed those, the whole deck would have eventually sheared right off the side of the hill. We had to send the entire load back to the fabricator in Ohio. And getting a flatbed that size back over the state lines with the new weight restrictions on the interstate... it was a complete logistical nightmare. The driver got held up at a weigh station for two days because his permits didn't match the axle load, and the client was practically breathing down my neck about the delays. \n\nWait... sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking if my jaw can be fixed. I just... I honestly don't know. I don't know if anything could actually make it look normal. I just know I can't stand it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your jaw and mouth, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did?\n\nPatient: Oh, they definitely notice it. I mean... I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this awful droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every photo, every reflection... I catch sight of myself in a window and it just ruins my whole day. And people, you know, my husband or my sister, they'll tell me I look fine, but it feels so incredibly obvious to me. It's so bad that I've started avoiding presentations and clients, like I said earlier. \n\nActually, speaking of reflections, the glazing on this new office building we're working on downtown is just completely wrong. We ordered this low-iron, ultra-clear glass so the lobby would feel completely transparent, like there's no barrier between the street and the interior. But the manufacturer messed up the tempering process, and now there's this slight roller wave distortion in every single pane. When you walk past it on the sidewalk, the reflection kind of warps and wobbles, almost like a funhouse mirror. It drives me absolutely crazy. I told the supplier we're not accepting the shipment, but they're trying to claim it's within industry tolerances. Industry tolerances! As if I'm going to let my firm's name be attached to a building that looks like it's melting. I've been on the phone with their regional manager for three days straight, and he just keeps talking in circles about thermal stress and wind loads... \n\nOh, gosh. I'm sorry. The glass just gets me so worked up. What were you asking? Right, if people notice my face. Yeah, I just... even if they say they don't, I know they see how uneven it is. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would go back to normal if your jaw and mouth looked exactly the way you want them to?\n\nPatient: I don't even know what normal is anymore. If it looked exactly the way I wanted... I mean, I guess? But I'm not really sure. Sometimes you get exactly what you want and it doesn't actually fix the whole picture, you know? \n\nLike, we had this client last year, this couple buying a place in the historic district, and they wanted their kitchen remodel exactly to this very specific mid-century spec. They spent months sourcing these original teak cabinets from Denmark, cost an absolute fortune, completely blew the budget. But when my crew pulled up the old linoleum to prep for the new tile, the entire subfloor was completely rotted out. There had been this slow pipe leak that had probably been dripping since the late eighties. We had to sister all the floor joists, and the husband was just furious because it pushed the timeline back by three months. But you can't just install perfect, expensive teak cabinets on a rotting floor. The whole house would eventually smell like mildew. And getting the revised permits for historic districts takes forever anyway. The lady at the zoning office, Brenda, she scrutinizes every single application like she's guarding the crown jewels, and she makes you submit everything in triplicate...\n\nOh, God. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. My brain just... it just goes there. \n\nYou were asking if fixing my jaw would make my life normal again. I just... I honestly don't know. I can't even picture what my life was like before I started obsessing over this. I'd like to think it would fix everything, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about your jaw and mouth if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in reflections or photos?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess if I couldn't see myself at all, maybe I wouldn't think about it as much? But I feel like I'd still just *know* it was there. It's like this weight on me. \n\nAlthough, speaking of being completely alone without seeing anything... it reminds me of this site survey I had to do last winter up in the mountains. We were looking at this plot for an off-grid retreat center, and I got separated from the surveyor when this sudden whiteout blizzard hit. I was stuck in my truck for six hours, completely alone, couldn't see two feet past the windshield. The snow was piling up so fast I thought I was going to be buried alive. The heater in that old Ford F-250 barely works on a good day, and the blower motor was making this horrible screeching sound. I had to keep turning it off so it wouldn't burn out, and I was freezing. I tried calling my husband, but there's absolutely no cell service up there past the county line. The county actually voted down the new cell tower proposal because the local bird-watching society claimed it would disrupt the migration pattern of some rare owl. So I just sat there, shivering, eating stale granola bars I found in the glovebox...\n\nSorry. I'm drifting again. Um, if I was alone and couldn't see my face... I don't know. I think the feeling of it being wrong would still just be there, even in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be worried about what other people think of you if it weren't for your concerns about your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: Would I worry about what people think otherwise? I... I'm not really sure. I don't think so? I mean, you always have to care a little bit about what people think in my line of work, right? \n\nLike, we had this public consultation last month for a new mixed-use development by the riverfront, and I was so stressed about the community feedback. We spent weeks rendering these beautiful pedestrian walkways, and this one guy, this retired accountant or something, just monopolized the entire microphone for twenty minutes complaining about the brick color. We chose a classic Chicago common brick to match the historic warehouses next door, but he insisted it looked like dried ketchup. Dried ketchup! And then he started going off about the parking ratio, which isn't even my department, that's civil engineering. But the lead civil engineer, Dave, was out on paternity leave because his wife just had twins—a boy and a girl, actually, which is sweet, but Dave left his project files an absolute mess. I couldn't find the traffic impact study anywhere on the firm's shared drive. I spent three hours clicking through folders named things like \"Final_Final_v2\" just trying to find the parking metrics so I could answer this guy...\n\nOh, wow. I am so sorry. I keep doing this today, don't I? My mind just races. \n\nWhat I mean is... I really don't know if I'd be worried about other things. It's hard to even imagine not worrying about my mouth and my jaw. It just takes up all the space in my head, so I honestly don't know what else I'd be worrying about if it was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your jaw and mouth are more about how you feel about them yourself, or about what you think others might think of them?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, it's both, right? I hate how it looks, but I also know everyone else must be staring at it. It's just so obvious. \n\nWhich is kind of like this situation we had with a load-bearing wall on a residential job last spring. The clients wanted this completely open-concept first floor, right? Just one massive space. But they bought this old Victorian, and you can't just knock down a central wall without putting in a massive steel I-beam. I told them it would drop the ceiling height by at least eight inches in the middle of the room, and it would be really obvious. They said they didn't care, they just wanted the open space. So we order the beam, get the crane out there, and the crane operator—this guy named Gary who always wears these incredibly bright neon yellow shirts, like, aggressively bright—he accidentally swings the beam too wide and clips the neighbor's oak tree. The neighbor was this retired judge who threatened to sue the entire firm over a broken branch. We had to get our legal department involved, and it turned into this whole mediation thing that dragged on for weeks. And after all that, when we finally got the beam installed and boxed it in with drywall, the clients hated it. They said it looked like a giant scar across the ceiling. I told them it was going to be obvious, but they just couldn't visualize it until it was right there, ruining the flow of the room...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I don't know which is worse, me hating it or knowing everyone else sees it. I honestly can't tell you. It just all feels terrible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth actually look the way you describe them to others, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, they *do* look that way. I'm not making it up. I look in the mirror and it's just... it's right there. It's not just a feeling, it's a physical reality. \n\nIt's like when you're looking at a blueprint and you can just see that the scale is off. You know? Like, I had this intern last summer, sweet kid from the state college, but he had absolutely no spatial awareness. I asked him to draft a preliminary layout for a small cafe renovation, and he put the ADA-compliant restroom right next to the kitchen prep area, but he didn't account for the door swing. If you opened the bathroom door, it would literally hit the espresso machine. I tried to explain to him that you can't just draw boxes on a screen, you have to imagine people actually moving through the space. But he just kept staring at his CAD software like the computer was going to fix it for him. Kids these days rely so much on the software, they don't even know how to hand-draft anymore. When I was in school, we spent the entire first year just doing hand-lettering and orthographic projections. My drafting professor, Mr. Henderson, he used to walk around with this red pen and if your line weight was even slightly off, he'd draw a giant X across your entire vellum sheet. It was brutal, but you learned to see things exactly as they were. You couldn't hide a mistake.\n\nUm... sorry. What I mean is, I'm not just imagining it. I see the mistake. It's right there on my face, and it's real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your jaw and mouth might look normal to others, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Have I considered that? I mean, people do tell me I look fine. My husband says it, my sister says it. But... I just can't see it that way. I'm here because I can't stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. It feels so glaringly obvious that I've actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nLike, last month, I completely backed out of pitching this boutique hotel renovation downtown. I made my junior partner do it instead. Which was a massive risk, because the hotel owners are these notoriously difficult brothers who made their money in wholesale plumbing supplies. They were obsessed with the bathroom fixtures, obviously. They wanted these imported Italian rainfall showerheads that require a completely different water pressure system than what the century-old building could handle. I tried to explain in our early emails that the plumbing retrofits would cost a fortune, but they wouldn't listen. We had to bring in a specialized contractor, this guy named Sal who drives a van with a giant cartoon wrench painted on the side. Sal took one look at the existing copper pipes and just started laughing. It took three weeks just to repipe the third floor, and the drywall dust got absolutely everywhere. The cleaning crew threatened to walk off the job because the dust kept settling on the antique mahogany wainscoting in the lobby...\n\nOh, gosh. I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? \n\nWhat I'm trying to say is... even when people tell me it looks normal, I just don't believe them. The unevenness is just so obvious to me. I see it in every mirror, every window I walk past. That's why I'm here. I can't stop seeing it, and it's getting to the point where I can't even stand in front of a room of clients anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a plastic surgeon or a dentist could fix the issues you see with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I've thought about it. I've spent hours looking up procedures, wondering if maybe a facelift or some kind of jaw reconstruction would finally fix it. But then I worry that they wouldn't see exactly what I see, and they'd just make it worse. \n\nIt's like when you hire a subcontractor who thinks they know better than the blueprints. We had this masonry guy last fall, working on a custom brick patio for a client in the suburbs. The plans specifically called for a herringbone pattern with a very specific charcoal mortar. But this guy, he decides that a standard running bond would be faster, and he uses this bright white mortar that looked absolutely garish against the reclaimed brick. When I got to the site and saw what he was doing, I nearly lost my mind. I had to make him rip up half the patio, and of course, he tried to charge us for the extra labor. He claimed the herringbone pattern was too complicated for the grade of the yard, which was complete nonsense. We ended up having to eat the cost of the ruined bricks just to keep the clients happy, and they were already stressed because their golden retriever had just had surgery and they couldn't let the dog out back...\n\nSorry. I just... I don't know if a surgeon could fix it. I don't trust that they'd understand how bad it is, and what if I go through all that and it still looks wrong? Or worse? I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal to you because of how they actually appear or because of how you feel about their appearance?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I think it's how they actually appear. It has to be. I look in the mirror and the asymmetry is just a physical fact. It's not a feeling, it's a structural flaw. \n\nIt's exactly like when you're looking at a load-bearing wall that's starting to bow. You don't \"feel\" like it's bowing, you can see the drywall cracking. We had this project a few years ago, a mid-century modern restoration up in the hills. Beautiful property, all glass and exposed beams, but the previous owners had tried to open up the floor plan themselves. They took out this crucial support pillar in the living room because they wanted an unobstructed view of the valley. Total amateurs. They didn't even consult an engineer. So the roof line started sagging, and by the time we got the contract, you could literally put a marble on the floor and watch it roll straight toward the center of the room. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams just to stabilize the structure before we could even think about the aesthetics. The crane operator had a terrible time getting the beams up the narrow, winding driveway. He ended up backing into the neighbor's vintage mailbox, which was this whole separate disaster because the neighbor was a retired lawyer who threatened to sue the city over the zoning permits...\n\nUm, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The structural flaw. I just... I don't think it's in my head. I think my jaw and my mouth are just physically wrong, and I can't unsee it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we keep going in circles, and I'm sorry. But it is actually there. Like I was trying to say earlier, I'm here in your office because I literally cannot stop seeing this droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection I catch of myself. Even when my husband or whoever tells me I look fine, I know what I'm seeing. It's so glaringly obvious to me that it's the whole reason I've started completely avoiding my clients and skipping presentations. I just can't bear to be looked at. 

Especially near mirrors. God, mirrors are just the worst. We actually just finished a high-end Pilates studio downtown, and the client insisted on seamless, floor-to-ceiling mirrors on all four walls. Do you have any idea what a nightmare that is for lighting design? If you don't angle the recessed fixtures perfectly, you get this infinite glare loop that gives everyone an instant migraine. Plus, getting those massive glass panels delivered... the freight driver got completely lost and tried to turn his 18-wheeler around in a tiny residential cul-de-sac. He ended up backing right over this poor woman's prized hydrangea bushes. She came out of her house screaming, practically waving a garden hose at him. The police had to be called, and it delayed our installation by a week and a half, which meant the flooring guys had to reschedule, and they were already booked out for months because of supply chain issues with the bamboo... 

Wait, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's because it's actually there. I'm not just imagining the unevenness. It's real, and it's ruining my career.

You have to use these massive thermal blankets, and they're so heavy to lug around the site... \n\nWait, sorry. You were asking about my life. Right. \n\nUm... well, besides the presentations, I've also been missing all this award-related networking stuff because I just can't face being seen by anyone in the industry. It's too humiliating. So my project deadlines are starting to completely fall apart because I'm just hiding out. And my family... I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister just had a big barbecue for my niece's graduation, and they catered from that place on 5th street with the amazing brisket. They smoke it over hickory for like fourteen hours, the bark on it is just incredible. I used to go there every Tuesday for lunch before they got super popular and raised their prices. \n\nBut anyway, I just... I couldn't go to the party. I stayed home. I just can't let people look at me right now, not even my own family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and mouth could be fixed or improved in some way, or do you see them as inherently flawed?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I'm not sure if it can actually be fixed, or if the underlying structure is just... wrong. Like it's just built wrong. \n\nIt's funny, when you use the phrase \"inherently flawed,\" it makes me think of this structural steel we got delivered for a cantilevered deck last spring. The mill actually sent us beams with these tiny micro-fractures in the flanges. You couldn't even see them with the naked eye, but the inspector caught it on the ultrasound test. If we had installed those, the whole deck would have eventually sheared right off the side of the hill. We had to send the entire load back to the fabricator in Ohio. And getting a flatbed that size back over the state lines with the new weight restrictions on the interstate... it was a complete logistical nightmare. The driver got held up at a weigh station for two days because his permits didn't match the axle load, and the client was practically breathing down my neck about the delays. \n\nWait... sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking if my jaw can be fixed. I just... I honestly don't know. I don't know if anything could actually make it look normal. I just know I can't stand it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your jaw and mouth, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did?\n\nPatient: Oh, they definitely notice it. I mean... I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this awful droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every photo, every reflection... I catch sight of myself in a window and it just ruins my whole day. And people, you know, my husband or my sister, they'll tell me I look fine, but it feels so incredibly obvious to me. It's so bad that I've started avoiding presentations and clients, like I said earlier. \n\nActually, speaking of reflections, the glazing on this new office building we're working on downtown is just completely wrong. We ordered this low-iron, ultra-clear glass so the lobby would feel completely transparent, like there's no barrier between the street and the interior. But the manufacturer messed up the tempering process, and now there's this slight roller wave distortion in every single pane. When you walk past it on the sidewalk, the reflection kind of warps and wobbles, almost like a funhouse mirror. It drives me absolutely crazy. I told the supplier we're not accepting the shipment, but they're trying to claim it's within industry tolerances. Industry tolerances! As if I'm going to let my firm's name be attached to a building that looks like it's melting. I've been on the phone with their regional manager for three days straight, and he just keeps talking in circles about thermal stress and wind loads... \n\nOh, gosh. I'm sorry. The glass just gets me so worked up. What were you asking? Right, if people notice my face. Yeah, I just... even if they say they don't, I know they see how uneven it is. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would go back to normal if your jaw and mouth looked exactly the way you want them to?\n\nPatient: I don't even know what normal is anymore. If it looked exactly the way I wanted... I mean, I guess? But I'm not really sure. Sometimes you get exactly what you want and it doesn't actually fix the whole picture, you know? \n\nLike, we had this client last year, this couple buying a place in the historic district, and they wanted their kitchen remodel exactly to this very specific mid-century spec. They spent months sourcing these original teak cabinets from Denmark, cost an absolute fortune, completely blew the budget. But when my crew pulled up the old linoleum to prep for the new tile, the entire subfloor was completely rotted out. There had been this slow pipe leak that had probably been dripping since the late eighties. We had to sister all the floor joists, and the husband was just furious because it pushed the timeline back by three months. But you can't just install perfect, expensive teak cabinets on a rotting floor. The whole house would eventually smell like mildew. And getting the revised permits for historic districts takes forever anyway. The lady at the zoning office, Brenda, she scrutinizes every single application like she's guarding the crown jewels, and she makes you submit everything in triplicate...\n\nOh, God. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. My brain just... it just goes there. \n\nYou were asking if fixing my jaw would make my life normal again. I just... I honestly don't know. I can't even picture what my life was like before I started obsessing over this. I'd like to think it would fix everything, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about your jaw and mouth if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in reflections or photos?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess if I couldn't see myself at all, maybe I wouldn't think about it as much? But I feel like I'd still just *know* it was there. It's like this weight on me. \n\nAlthough, speaking of being completely alone without seeing anything... it reminds me of this site survey I had to do last winter up in the mountains. We were looking at this plot for an off-grid retreat center, and I got separated from the surveyor when this sudden whiteout blizzard hit. I was stuck in my truck for six hours, completely alone, couldn't see two feet past the windshield. The snow was piling up so fast I thought I was going to be buried alive. The heater in that old Ford F-250 barely works on a good day, and the blower motor was making this horrible screeching sound. I had to keep turning it off so it wouldn't burn out, and I was freezing. I tried calling my husband, but there's absolutely no cell service up there past the county line. The county actually voted down the new cell tower proposal because the local bird-watching society claimed it would disrupt the migration pattern of some rare owl. So I just sat there, shivering, eating stale granola bars I found in the glovebox...\n\nSorry. I'm drifting again. Um, if I was alone and couldn't see my face... I don't know. I think the feeling of it being wrong would still just be there, even in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be worried about what other people think of you if it weren't for your concerns about your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: Would I worry about what people think otherwise? I... I'm not really sure. I don't think so? I mean, you always have to care a little bit about what people think in my line of work, right? \n\nLike, we had this public consultation last month for a new mixed-use development by the riverfront, and I was so stressed about the community feedback. We spent weeks rendering these beautiful pedestrian walkways, and this one guy, this retired accountant or something, just monopolized the entire microphone for twenty minutes complaining about the brick color. We chose a classic Chicago common brick to match the historic warehouses next door, but he insisted it looked like dried ketchup. Dried ketchup! And then he started going off about the parking ratio, which isn't even my department, that's civil engineering. But the lead civil engineer, Dave, was out on paternity leave because his wife just had twins—a boy and a girl, actually, which is sweet, but Dave left his project files an absolute mess. I couldn't find the traffic impact study anywhere on the firm's shared drive. I spent three hours clicking through folders named things like \"Final_Final_v2\" just trying to find the parking metrics so I could answer this guy...\n\nOh, wow. I am so sorry. I keep doing this today, don't I? My mind just races. \n\nWhat I mean is... I really don't know if I'd be worried about other things. It's hard to even imagine not worrying about my mouth and my jaw. It just takes up all the space in my head, so I honestly don't know what else I'd be worrying about if it was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your jaw and mouth are more about how you feel about them yourself, or about what you think others might think of them?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, it's both, right? I hate how it looks, but I also know everyone else must be staring at it. It's just so obvious. \n\nWhich is kind of like this situation we had with a load-bearing wall on a residential job last spring. The clients wanted this completely open-concept first floor, right? Just one massive space. But they bought this old Victorian, and you can't just knock down a central wall without putting in a massive steel I-beam. I told them it would drop the ceiling height by at least eight inches in the middle of the room, and it would be really obvious. They said they didn't care, they just wanted the open space. So we order the beam, get the crane out there, and the crane operator—this guy named Gary who always wears these incredibly bright neon yellow shirts, like, aggressively bright—he accidentally swings the beam too wide and clips the neighbor's oak tree. The neighbor was this retired judge who threatened to sue the entire firm over a broken branch. We had to get our legal department involved, and it turned into this whole mediation thing that dragged on for weeks. And after all that, when we finally got the beam installed and boxed it in with drywall, the clients hated it. They said it looked like a giant scar across the ceiling. I told them it was going to be obvious, but they just couldn't visualize it until it was right there, ruining the flow of the room...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I don't know which is worse, me hating it or knowing everyone else sees it. I honestly can't tell you. It just all feels terrible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth actually look the way you describe them to others, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, they *do* look that way. I'm not making it up. I look in the mirror and it's just... it's right there. It's not just a feeling, it's a physical reality. \n\nIt's like when you're looking at a blueprint and you can just see that the scale is off. You know? Like, I had this intern last summer, sweet kid from the state college, but he had absolutely no spatial awareness. I asked him to draft a preliminary layout for a small cafe renovation, and he put the ADA-compliant restroom right next to the kitchen prep area, but he didn't account for the door swing. If you opened the bathroom door, it would literally hit the espresso machine. I tried to explain to him that you can't just draw boxes on a screen, you have to imagine people actually moving through the space. But he just kept staring at his CAD software like the computer was going to fix it for him. Kids these days rely so much on the software, they don't even know how to hand-draft anymore. When I was in school, we spent the entire first year just doing hand-lettering and orthographic projections. My drafting professor, Mr. Henderson, he used to walk around with this red pen and if your line weight was even slightly off, he'd draw a giant X across your entire vellum sheet. It was brutal, but you learned to see things exactly as they were. You couldn't hide a mistake.\n\nUm... sorry. What I mean is, I'm not just imagining it. I see the mistake. It's right there on my face, and it's real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your jaw and mouth might look normal to others, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Have I considered that? I mean, people do tell me I look fine. My husband says it, my sister says it. But... I just can't see it that way. I'm here because I can't stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. It feels so glaringly obvious that I've actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nLike, last month, I completely backed out of pitching this boutique hotel renovation downtown. I made my junior partner do it instead. Which was a massive risk, because the hotel owners are these notoriously difficult brothers who made their money in wholesale plumbing supplies. They were obsessed with the bathroom fixtures, obviously. They wanted these imported Italian rainfall showerheads that require a completely different water pressure system than what the century-old building could handle. I tried to explain in our early emails that the plumbing retrofits would cost a fortune, but they wouldn't listen. We had to bring in a specialized contractor, this guy named Sal who drives a van with a giant cartoon wrench painted on the side. Sal took one look at the existing copper pipes and just started laughing. It took three weeks just to repipe the third floor, and the drywall dust got absolutely everywhere. The cleaning crew threatened to walk off the job because the dust kept settling on the antique mahogany wainscoting in the lobby...\n\nOh, gosh. I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? \n\nWhat I'm trying to say is... even when people tell me it looks normal, I just don't believe them. The unevenness is just so obvious to me. I see it in every mirror, every window I walk past. That's why I'm here. I can't stop seeing it, and it's getting to the point where I can't even stand in front of a room of clients anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a plastic surgeon or a dentist could fix the issues you see with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I've thought about it. I've spent hours looking up procedures, wondering if maybe a facelift or some kind of jaw reconstruction would finally fix it. But then I worry that they wouldn't see exactly what I see, and they'd just make it worse. \n\nIt's like when you hire a subcontractor who thinks they know better than the blueprints. We had this masonry guy last fall, working on a custom brick patio for a client in the suburbs. The plans specifically called for a herringbone pattern with a very specific charcoal mortar. But this guy, he decides that a standard running bond would be faster, and he uses this bright white mortar that looked absolutely garish against the reclaimed brick. When I got to the site and saw what he was doing, I nearly lost my mind. I had to make him rip up half the patio, and of course, he tried to charge us for the extra labor. He claimed the herringbone pattern was too complicated for the grade of the yard, which was complete nonsense. We ended up having to eat the cost of the ruined bricks just to keep the clients happy, and they were already stressed because their golden retriever had just had surgery and they couldn't let the dog out back...\n\nSorry. I just... I don't know if a surgeon could fix it. I don't trust that they'd understand how bad it is, and what if I go through all that and it still looks wrong? Or worse? I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal to you because of how they actually appear or because of how you feel about their appearance?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I think it's how they actually appear. It has to be. I look in the mirror and the asymmetry is just a physical fact. It's not a feeling, it's a structural flaw. \n\nIt's exactly like when you're looking at a load-bearing wall that's starting to bow. You don't \"feel\" like it's bowing, you can see the drywall cracking. We had this project a few years ago, a mid-century modern restoration up in the hills. Beautiful property, all glass and exposed beams, but the previous owners had tried to open up the floor plan themselves. They took out this crucial support pillar in the living room because they wanted an unobstructed view of the valley. Total amateurs. They didn't even consult an engineer. So the roof line started sagging, and by the time we got the contract, you could literally put a marble on the floor and watch it roll straight toward the center of the room. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams just to stabilize the structure before we could even think about the aesthetics. The crane operator had a terrible time getting the beams up the narrow, winding driveway. He ended up backing into the neighbor's vintage mailbox, which was this whole separate disaster because the neighbor was a retired lawyer who threatened to sue the city over the zoning permits...\n\nUm, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The structural flaw. I just... I don't think it's in my head. I think my jaw and my mouth are just physically wrong, and I can't unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal because of something that is actually there or because of what you think is there?\n\nPatient: I know we keep going in circles, and I'm sorry. But it is actually there. Like I was trying to say earlier, I'm here in your office because I literally cannot stop seeing this droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection I catch of myself. Even when my husband or whoever tells me I look fine, I know what I'm seeing. It's so glaringly obvious to me that it's the whole reason I've started completely avoiding my clients and skipping presentations. I just can't bear to be looked at. \n\nEspecially near mirrors. God, mirrors are just the worst. We actually just finished a high-end Pilates studio downtown, and the client insisted on seamless, floor-to-ceiling mirrors on all four walls. Plus, getting those massive glass panels delivered... the freight driver got completely lost and tried to turn his 18-wheeler around in a tiny residential cul-de-sac. He ended up backing right over this poor woman's prized hydrangea bushes. She came out of her house screaming, practically waving a garden hose at him. The police had to be called, and it delayed our installation by a week and a half, which meant the flooring guys had to reschedule, and they were already booked out for months because of supply chain issues with the bamboo... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's because it's actually there. I'm not just imagining the unevenness. It's real, and it's ruining my career.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know. I mean, I would hope they'd see it, right? They're trained to look at symmetry and proportions. But then again, maybe they wouldn't. Maybe they'd just look at me and think I'm crazy, or they'd try to sell me on some other procedure I don't even need.

And then the plumber got so offended he threatened to walk off the job, right in the middle of rough-in. We had to bribe him with premium parking just to get him to finish the bathrooms...

Sorry, I'm rambling again. It's just... I really don't know what a surgeon would say. I'm terrified they wouldn't see the droop, or that they'd just dismiss me.

Even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so glaringly obvious to me. It's gotten so bad that I've actually started avoiding my presentations and my clients altogether. I just can't sit in a room and let them stare at my face looking like this.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just my mouth and my jaw. Just that whole lower section of my face. Honestly, I used to love wearing those masks on the job sites a couple of years ago. I kept wearing mine way longer than anyone else did just so I could cover it up. Dave—my contractor, the one I was just talking about—he used to joke that I was being overly cautious about the drywall dust. \n\nBut honestly, drywall dust is an absolute nightmare to get out of your clothes. We were doing a level five smooth finish on this massive gallery wall for a client last month, and with a level five, you have to skim coat the entire surface so it's perfectly flat. If the natural light hits it at the wrong angle, you see every single tiny bump or shadow. It takes forever to get right. The clients are this young couple who just moved here from Silicon Valley, and they just could not understand why it was taking an extra week. They kept texting me at all hours—like, 11 PM on a Sunday—asking why the painters weren't in yet. I had to explain to them, you can't just slap primer on unfinished drywall if you're planning to hang high-end art, it just doesn't work that way... \n\nI'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about my face. No, it's just my jaw and my mouth. I don't really think about the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your daily life or relationships due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it's, um, it's ruining everything, honestly. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the very last minute the other day because I just... I couldn't bear the thought of them staring at my jaw. Which is terrible, because it was for this incredible brutalist revival project we're pitching. We're using poured-in-place concrete for the interior walls, and let me tell you, concrete is just so unforgiving. If the wooden forms aren't perfectly sanded and aligned, you get these awful seam lines and textures that you just cannot hide. My foreman is usually a wizard with it, but the temperature dropped last week and curing concrete in the cold is a complete nightmare. You have to use these massive thermal blankets, and they're so heavy to lug around the site... \n\nWait, sorry. You were asking about my life. Right. \n\nUm... well, besides the presentations, I've also been missing all this award-related networking stuff because I just can't face being seen by anyone in the industry. It's too humiliating. So my project deadlines are starting to completely fall apart because I'm just hiding out. And my family... I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister just had a big barbecue for my niece's graduation, and they catered from that place on 5th street with the amazing brisket. They smoke it over hickory for like fourteen hours, the bark on it is just incredible. I used to go there every Tuesday for lunch before they got super popular and raised their prices. \n\nBut anyway, I just... I couldn't go to the party. I stayed home. I just can't let people look at me right now, not even my own family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and mouth could be fixed or improved in some way, or do you see them as inherently flawed?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I'm not sure if it can actually be fixed, or if the underlying structure is just... wrong. Like it's just built wrong. \n\nIt's funny, when you use the phrase \"inherently flawed,\" it makes me think of this structural steel we got delivered for a cantilevered deck last spring. The mill actually sent us beams with these tiny micro-fractures in the flanges. You couldn't even see them with the naked eye, but the inspector caught it on the ultrasound test. If we had installed those, the whole deck would have eventually sheared right off the side of the hill. We had to send the entire load back to the fabricator in Ohio. And getting a flatbed that size back over the state lines with the new weight restrictions on the interstate... it was a complete logistical nightmare. The driver got held up at a weigh station for two days because his permits didn't match the axle load, and the client was practically breathing down my neck about the delays. \n\nWait... sorry. I'm doing it again. You were asking if my jaw can be fixed. I just... I honestly don't know. I don't know if anything could actually make it look normal. I just know I can't stand it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people actually notice your jaw and mouth, or is it more that you're afraid of what they might think if they did?\n\nPatient: Oh, they definitely notice it. I mean... I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this awful droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every photo, every reflection... I catch sight of myself in a window and it just ruins my whole day. And people, you know, my husband or my sister, they'll tell me I look fine, but it feels so incredibly obvious to me. It's so bad that I've started avoiding presentations and clients, like I said earlier. \n\nActually, speaking of reflections, the glazing on this new office building we're working on downtown is just completely wrong. We ordered this low-iron, ultra-clear glass so the lobby would feel completely transparent, like there's no barrier between the street and the interior. But the manufacturer messed up the tempering process, and now there's this slight roller wave distortion in every single pane. When you walk past it on the sidewalk, the reflection kind of warps and wobbles, almost like a funhouse mirror. It drives me absolutely crazy. I told the supplier we're not accepting the shipment, but they're trying to claim it's within industry tolerances. Industry tolerances! As if I'm going to let my firm's name be attached to a building that looks like it's melting. I've been on the phone with their regional manager for three days straight, and he just keeps talking in circles about thermal stress and wind loads... \n\nOh, gosh. I'm sorry. The glass just gets me so worked up. What were you asking? Right, if people notice my face. Yeah, I just... even if they say they don't, I know they see how uneven it is. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would go back to normal if your jaw and mouth looked exactly the way you want them to?\n\nPatient: I don't even know what normal is anymore. If it looked exactly the way I wanted... I mean, I guess? But I'm not really sure. Sometimes you get exactly what you want and it doesn't actually fix the whole picture, you know? \n\nLike, we had this client last year, this couple buying a place in the historic district, and they wanted their kitchen remodel exactly to this very specific mid-century spec. They spent months sourcing these original teak cabinets from Denmark, cost an absolute fortune, completely blew the budget. But when my crew pulled up the old linoleum to prep for the new tile, the entire subfloor was completely rotted out. There had been this slow pipe leak that had probably been dripping since the late eighties. We had to sister all the floor joists, and the husband was just furious because it pushed the timeline back by three months. But you can't just install perfect, expensive teak cabinets on a rotting floor. The whole house would eventually smell like mildew. And getting the revised permits for historic districts takes forever anyway. The lady at the zoning office, Brenda, she scrutinizes every single application like she's guarding the crown jewels, and she makes you submit everything in triplicate...\n\nOh, God. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. My brain just... it just goes there. \n\nYou were asking if fixing my jaw would make my life normal again. I just... I honestly don't know. I can't even picture what my life was like before I started obsessing over this. I'd like to think it would fix everything, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about your jaw and mouth if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in reflections or photos?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess if I couldn't see myself at all, maybe I wouldn't think about it as much? But I feel like I'd still just *know* it was there. It's like this weight on me. \n\nAlthough, speaking of being completely alone without seeing anything... it reminds me of this site survey I had to do last winter up in the mountains. We were looking at this plot for an off-grid retreat center, and I got separated from the surveyor when this sudden whiteout blizzard hit. I was stuck in my truck for six hours, completely alone, couldn't see two feet past the windshield. The snow was piling up so fast I thought I was going to be buried alive. The heater in that old Ford F-250 barely works on a good day, and the blower motor was making this horrible screeching sound. I had to keep turning it off so it wouldn't burn out, and I was freezing. I tried calling my husband, but there's absolutely no cell service up there past the county line. The county actually voted down the new cell tower proposal because the local bird-watching society claimed it would disrupt the migration pattern of some rare owl. So I just sat there, shivering, eating stale granola bars I found in the glovebox...\n\nSorry. I'm drifting again. Um, if I was alone and couldn't see my face... I don't know. I think the feeling of it being wrong would still just be there, even in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be worried about what other people think of you if it weren't for your concerns about your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: Would I worry about what people think otherwise? I... I'm not really sure. I don't think so? I mean, you always have to care a little bit about what people think in my line of work, right? \n\nLike, we had this public consultation last month for a new mixed-use development by the riverfront, and I was so stressed about the community feedback. We spent weeks rendering these beautiful pedestrian walkways, and this one guy, this retired accountant or something, just monopolized the entire microphone for twenty minutes complaining about the brick color. We chose a classic Chicago common brick to match the historic warehouses next door, but he insisted it looked like dried ketchup. Dried ketchup! And then he started going off about the parking ratio, which isn't even my department, that's civil engineering. But the lead civil engineer, Dave, was out on paternity leave because his wife just had twins—a boy and a girl, actually, which is sweet, but Dave left his project files an absolute mess. I couldn't find the traffic impact study anywhere on the firm's shared drive. I spent three hours clicking through folders named things like \"Final_Final_v2\" just trying to find the parking metrics so I could answer this guy...\n\nOh, wow. I am so sorry. I keep doing this today, don't I? My mind just races. \n\nWhat I mean is... I really don't know if I'd be worried about other things. It's hard to even imagine not worrying about my mouth and my jaw. It just takes up all the space in my head, so I honestly don't know what else I'd be worrying about if it was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your jaw and mouth are more about how you feel about them yourself, or about what you think others might think of them?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, it's both, right? I hate how it looks, but I also know everyone else must be staring at it. It's just so obvious. \n\nWhich is kind of like this situation we had with a load-bearing wall on a residential job last spring. The clients wanted this completely open-concept first floor, right? Just one massive space. But they bought this old Victorian, and you can't just knock down a central wall without putting in a massive steel I-beam. I told them it would drop the ceiling height by at least eight inches in the middle of the room, and it would be really obvious. They said they didn't care, they just wanted the open space. So we order the beam, get the crane out there, and the crane operator—this guy named Gary who always wears these incredibly bright neon yellow shirts, like, aggressively bright—he accidentally swings the beam too wide and clips the neighbor's oak tree. The neighbor was this retired judge who threatened to sue the entire firm over a broken branch. We had to get our legal department involved, and it turned into this whole mediation thing that dragged on for weeks. And after all that, when we finally got the beam installed and boxed it in with drywall, the clients hated it. They said it looked like a giant scar across the ceiling. I told them it was going to be obvious, but they just couldn't visualize it until it was right there, ruining the flow of the room...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I don't know which is worse, me hating it or knowing everyone else sees it. I honestly can't tell you. It just all feels terrible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth actually look the way you describe them to others, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, they *do* look that way. I'm not making it up. I look in the mirror and it's just... it's right there. It's not just a feeling, it's a physical reality. \n\nIt's like when you're looking at a blueprint and you can just see that the scale is off. You know? Like, I had this intern last summer, sweet kid from the state college, but he had absolutely no spatial awareness. I asked him to draft a preliminary layout for a small cafe renovation, and he put the ADA-compliant restroom right next to the kitchen prep area, but he didn't account for the door swing. If you opened the bathroom door, it would literally hit the espresso machine. I tried to explain to him that you can't just draw boxes on a screen, you have to imagine people actually moving through the space. But he just kept staring at his CAD software like the computer was going to fix it for him. Kids these days rely so much on the software, they don't even know how to hand-draft anymore. When I was in school, we spent the entire first year just doing hand-lettering and orthographic projections. My drafting professor, Mr. Henderson, he used to walk around with this red pen and if your line weight was even slightly off, he'd draw a giant X across your entire vellum sheet. It was brutal, but you learned to see things exactly as they were. You couldn't hide a mistake.\n\nUm... sorry. What I mean is, I'm not just imagining it. I see the mistake. It's right there on my face, and it's real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your jaw and mouth might look normal to others, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Have I considered that? I mean, people do tell me I look fine. My husband says it, my sister says it. But... I just can't see it that way. I'm here because I can't stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. It feels so glaringly obvious that I've actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nLike, last month, I completely backed out of pitching this boutique hotel renovation downtown. They were obsessed with the bathroom fixtures, obviously. They wanted these imported Italian rainfall showerheads that require a completely different water pressure system than what the century-old building could handle. I tried to explain in our early emails that the plumbing retrofits would cost a fortune, but they wouldn't listen. We had to bring in a specialized contractor, this guy named Sal who drives a van with a giant cartoon wrench painted on the side. Sal took one look at the existing copper pipes and just started laughing. It took three weeks just to repipe the third floor, and the drywall dust got absolutely everywhere. The cleaning crew threatened to walk off the job because the dust kept settling on the antique mahogany wainscoting in the lobby...\n\nOh, gosh. I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? \n\nWhat I'm trying to say is... even when people tell me it looks normal, I just don't believe them. The unevenness is just so obvious to me. I see it in every mirror, every window I walk past. That's why I'm here. I can't stop seeing it, and it's getting to the point where I can't even stand in front of a room of clients anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a plastic surgeon or a dentist could fix the issues you see with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I've thought about it. I've spent hours looking up procedures, wondering if maybe a facelift or some kind of jaw reconstruction would finally fix it. But then I worry that they wouldn't see exactly what I see, and they'd just make it worse. \n\nIt's like when you hire a subcontractor who thinks they know better than the blueprints. We had this masonry guy last fall, working on a custom brick patio for a client in the suburbs. The plans specifically called for a herringbone pattern with a very specific charcoal mortar. But this guy, he decides that a standard running bond would be faster, and he uses this bright white mortar that looked absolutely garish against the reclaimed brick. When I got to the site and saw what he was doing, I nearly lost my mind. I had to make him rip up half the patio, and of course, he tried to charge us for the extra labor. He claimed the herringbone pattern was too complicated for the grade of the yard, which was complete nonsense. We ended up having to eat the cost of the ruined bricks just to keep the clients happy, and they were already stressed because their golden retriever had just had surgery and they couldn't let the dog out back...\n\nSorry. I just... I don't know if a surgeon could fix it. I don't trust that they'd understand how bad it is, and what if I go through all that and it still looks wrong? Or worse? I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal to you because of how they actually appear or because of how you feel about their appearance?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I think it's how they actually appear. It has to be. I look in the mirror and the asymmetry is just a physical fact. It's not a feeling, it's a structural flaw. \n\nIt's exactly like when you're looking at a load-bearing wall that's starting to bow. You don't \"feel\" like it's bowing, you can see the drywall cracking. We had this project a few years ago, a mid-century modern restoration up in the hills. Beautiful property, all glass and exposed beams, but the previous owners had tried to open up the floor plan themselves. They took out this crucial support pillar in the living room because they wanted an unobstructed view of the valley. Total amateurs. They didn't even consult an engineer. So the roof line started sagging, and by the time we got the contract, you could literally put a marble on the floor and watch it roll straight toward the center of the room. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams just to stabilize the structure before we could even think about the aesthetics. The crane operator had a terrible time getting the beams up the narrow, winding driveway. He ended up backing into the neighbor's vintage mailbox, which was this whole separate disaster because the neighbor was a retired lawyer who threatened to sue the city over the zoning permits...\n\nUm, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The structural flaw. I just... I don't think it's in my head. I think my jaw and my mouth are just physically wrong, and I can't unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal because of something that is actually there or because of what you think is there?\n\nPatient: I know we keep going in circles, and I'm sorry. But it is actually there. Like I was trying to say earlier, I'm here in your office because I literally cannot stop seeing this droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection I catch of myself. Even when my husband or whoever tells me I look fine, I know what I'm seeing. It's so glaringly obvious to me that it's the whole reason I've started completely avoiding my clients and skipping presentations. I just can't bear to be looked at. \n\nEspecially near mirrors. God, mirrors are just the worst. We actually just finished a high-end Pilates studio downtown, and the client insisted on seamless, floor-to-ceiling mirrors on all four walls. Do you have any idea what a nightmare that is for lighting design? If you don't angle the recessed fixtures perfectly, you get this infinite glare loop that gives everyone an instant migraine. Plus, getting those massive glass panels delivered... the freight driver got completely lost and tried to turn his 18-wheeler around in a tiny residential cul-de-sac. He ended up backing right over this poor woman's prized hydrangea bushes. She came out of her house screaming, practically waving a garden hose at him. The police had to be called, and it delayed our installation by a week and a half, which meant the flooring guys had to reschedule, and they were already booked out for months because of supply chain issues with the bamboo... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's because it's actually there. I'm not just imagining the unevenness. It's real, and it's ruining my career.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional, such as a plastic surgeon, would agree that there is something physically wrong with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I would hope they'd see it, right? They're trained to look at symmetry and proportions. But then again, maybe they wouldn't. Maybe they'd just look at me and think I'm crazy, or they'd try to sell me on some other procedure I don't even need. \n\nYou know how some contractors are. You bring them in to fix a leaky faucet, and suddenly they're telling you the entire plumbing system needs to be replaced because the copper pipes are oxidizing. We had this plumber on a commercial build last spring—a really nice guy, actually, brought his own espresso machine to the site every day—but he was obsessed with upselling. We just needed standard PVC for the drainage, and he kept trying to convince the client that they needed this specialized, acoustic-dampening cast iron pipe because the building was near a train track. The client was this tech startup, all 20-somethings who didn't know anything about construction, so they almost fell for it. I had to pull the CEO aside and explain that the train only ran twice a day and it was a freight line, so the acoustic pipes were a complete waste of their venture capital. And then the plumber got so offended he threatened to walk off the job, right in the middle of rough-in. We had to bribe him with premium parking just to get him to finish the bathrooms...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. It's just... I really don't know what a surgeon would say. I'm terrified they wouldn't see the droop, or that they'd just dismiss me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I... I honestly don't know. A dentist, maybe? I haven't been to one in a while because I just can't stand the thought of them staring right at my mouth under those bright lights. 

Speaking of those lights, we actually designed a pediatric dental clinic last year, and sourcing those specific overhead examination fixtures is an absolute nightmare. The head dentist wanted the whole place to be space-themed, so he ordered this massive custom fiberglass rocket ship for the waiting room. But the fabricator completely messed up the dimensions, and it wouldn't fit through the front double doors. We literally had to take out the entire front window pane just to get the thing inside. It was the middle of November, freezing cold, wind just howling through the lobby. The poor receptionist was huddled under her desk with a tiny space heater that kept tripping the circuit breaker because the electrical rough-in wasn't even fully signed off yet. And then the glass guys charged us double for an emergency weekend replacement, which completely blew the contingency budget... 

Um, anyway. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just... I don't know if a professional would confirm it. I'd like to think they would see the asymmetry because it's so obvious to me, but I'm just too terrified to actually go and find out.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I really... I just don't know. You keep asking me, and I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. I mean, I see it, plain as day, but a professional? Maybe they would, maybe they wouldn't. 

It reminds me of this structural engineer we work with sometimes, Dave. You can point out a crack in a foundation that looks like the whole building is about to slide down a hill, and he'll just tap it with his pen, look at his clipboard, and tell you it's just "normal settling." But then you show him a tiny hairline fracture in a load-bearing column that you can barely even see without a magnifying glass, and he'll shut down the whole site. We had a project over in the warehouse district—this beautiful old brick building we were converting into lofts—and Dave found this microscopic issue with the original timber joists. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams to reinforce the entire second floor. Getting those beams up the freight elevator was a disaster. The elevator got stuck between floors for four hours with two of the steelworkers inside. One of them was claustrophobic and started having a panic attack, so the fire department had to come and pry the doors open with the jaws of life. The whole thing made the evening news. 

So... yeah. I don't know what a doctor would say. They see things differently than we do, I guess. I'm just so afraid they'll tell me I'm making it all up.

I tried to explain to him that you can't just draw boxes on a screen, you have to imagine people actually moving through the space. But he just kept staring at his CAD software like the computer was going to fix it for him. Kids these days rely so much on the software, they don't even know how to hand-draft anymore. When I was in school, we spent the entire first year just doing hand-lettering and orthographic projections. My drafting professor, Mr. Henderson, he used to walk around with this red pen and if your line weight was even slightly off, he'd draw a giant X across your entire vellum sheet. It was brutal, but you learned to see things exactly as they were. You couldn't hide a mistake.\n\nUm... sorry. What I mean is, I'm not just imagining it. I see the mistake. It's right there on my face, and it's real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your jaw and mouth might look normal to others, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Have I considered that? I mean, people do tell me I look fine. My husband says it, my sister says it. But... I just can't see it that way. I'm here because I can't stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. It feels so glaringly obvious that I've actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nLike, last month, I completely backed out of pitching this boutique hotel renovation downtown. I made my junior partner do it instead. Which was a massive risk, because the hotel owners are these notoriously difficult brothers who made their money in wholesale plumbing supplies. Sal took one look at the existing copper pipes and just started laughing. It took three weeks just to repipe the third floor, and the drywall dust got absolutely everywhere. The cleaning crew threatened to walk off the job because the dust kept settling on the antique mahogany wainscoting in the lobby...\n\nOh, gosh. I'm so sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? \n\nWhat I'm trying to say is... even when people tell me it looks normal, I just don't believe them. The unevenness is just so obvious to me. I can't stop seeing it, and it's getting to the point where I can't even stand in front of a room of clients anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a plastic surgeon or a dentist could fix the issues you see with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I've thought about it. I've spent hours looking up procedures, wondering if maybe a facelift or some kind of jaw reconstruction would finally fix it. But then I worry that they wouldn't see exactly what I see, and they'd just make it worse. \n\nIt's like when you hire a subcontractor who thinks they know better than the blueprints. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal to you because of how they actually appear or because of how you feel about their appearance?\n\nPatient: I... I mean, I think it's how they actually appear. It has to be. I look in the mirror and the asymmetry is just a physical fact. It's not a feeling, it's a structural flaw. \n\nIt's exactly like when you're looking at a load-bearing wall that's starting to bow. You don't \"feel\" like it's bowing, you can see the drywall cracking. We had this project a few years ago, a mid-century modern restoration up in the hills. Beautiful property, all glass and exposed beams, but the previous owners had tried to open up the floor plan themselves. They took out this crucial support pillar in the living room because they wanted an unobstructed view of the valley. Total amateurs. They didn't even consult an engineer. So the roof line started sagging, and by the time we got the contract, you could literally put a marble on the floor and watch it roll straight toward the center of the room. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams just to stabilize the structure before we could even think about the aesthetics. The crane operator had a terrible time getting the beams up the narrow, winding driveway. He ended up backing into the neighbor's vintage mailbox, which was this whole separate disaster because the neighbor was a retired lawyer who threatened to sue the city over the zoning permits...\n\nUm, wait, what was I saying? Oh, right. The structural flaw. I just... I don't think it's in my head. I think my jaw and my mouth are just physically wrong, and I can't unsee it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and mouth look abnormal because of something that is actually there or because of what you think is there?\n\nPatient: I know we keep going in circles, and I'm sorry. But it is actually there. Like I was trying to say earlier, I'm here in your office because I literally cannot stop seeing this droop and the unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection I catch of myself. Even when my husband or whoever tells me I look fine, I know what I'm seeing. It's so glaringly obvious to me that it's the whole reason I've started completely avoiding my clients and skipping presentations. I just can't bear to be looked at. \n\nEspecially near mirrors. God, mirrors are just the worst. We actually just finished a high-end Pilates studio downtown, and the client insisted on seamless, floor-to-ceiling mirrors on all four walls. Do you have any idea what a nightmare that is for lighting design? If you don't angle the recessed fixtures perfectly, you get this infinite glare loop that gives everyone an instant migraine. Plus, getting those massive glass panels delivered... the freight driver got completely lost and tried to turn his 18-wheeler around in a tiny residential cul-de-sac. He ended up backing right over this poor woman's prized hydrangea bushes. She came out of her house screaming, practically waving a garden hose at him. The police had to be called, and it delayed our installation by a week and a half, which meant the flooring guys had to reschedule, and they were already booked out for months because of supply chain issues with the bamboo... \n\nWait, I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. But yes, to answer your question, it's because it's actually there. I'm not just imagining the unevenness. It's real, and it's ruining my career.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional, such as a plastic surgeon, would agree that there is something physically wrong with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, I would hope they'd see it, right? They're trained to look at symmetry and proportions. But then again, maybe they wouldn't. Maybe they'd just look at me and think I'm crazy, or they'd try to sell me on some other procedure I don't even need. \n\nYou know how some contractors are. You bring them in to fix a leaky faucet, and suddenly they're telling you the entire plumbing system needs to be replaced because the copper pipes are oxidizing. We had this plumber on a commercial build last spring—a really nice guy, actually, brought his own espresso machine to the site every day—but he was obsessed with upselling. We just needed standard PVC for the drainage, and he kept trying to convince the client that they needed this specialized, acoustic-dampening cast iron pipe because the building was near a train track. The client was this tech startup, all 20-somethings who didn't know anything about construction, so they almost fell for it. I had to pull the CEO aside and explain that the train only ran twice a day and it was a freight line, so the acoustic pipes were a complete waste of their venture capital. And then the plumber got so offended he threatened to walk off the job, right in the middle of rough-in. We had to bribe him with premium parking just to get him to finish the bathrooms...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. It's just... I really don't know what a surgeon would say. I'm terrified they wouldn't see the droop, or that they'd just dismiss me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation, such as from a plastic surgeon or a dentist, would confirm that there is a physical issue with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I... I honestly don't know. A dentist, maybe? I haven't been to one in a while because I just can't stand the thought of them staring right at my mouth under those bright lights. \n\nSpeaking of those lights, we actually designed a pediatric dental clinic last year, and sourcing those specific overhead examination fixtures is an absolute nightmare. The head dentist wanted the whole place to be space-themed, so he ordered this massive custom fiberglass rocket ship for the waiting room. But the fabricator completely messed up the dimensions, and it wouldn't fit through the front double doors. We literally had to take out the entire front window pane just to get the thing inside. It was the middle of November, freezing cold, wind just howling through the lobby. The poor receptionist was huddled under her desk with a tiny space heater that kept tripping the circuit breaker because the electrical rough-in wasn't even fully signed off yet. And then the glass guys charged us double for an emergency weekend replacement, which completely blew the contingency budget... \n\nUm, anyway. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just... I don't know if a professional would confirm it. I'd like to think they would see the asymmetry because it's so obvious to me, but I'm just too terrified to actually go and find out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation from a plastic surgeon or a dentist would find any physical issue with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I really... I just don't know. You keep asking me, and I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. I mean, I see it, plain as day, but a professional? Maybe they would, maybe they wouldn't. \n\nIt reminds me of this structural engineer we work with sometimes, Dave. You can point out a crack in a foundation that looks like the whole building is about to slide down a hill, and he'll just tap it with his pen, look at his clipboard, and tell you it's just \"normal settling.\" But then you show him a tiny hairline fracture in a load-bearing column that you can barely even see without a magnifying glass, and he'll shut down the whole site. We had a project over in the warehouse district—this beautiful old brick building we were converting into lofts—and Dave found this microscopic issue with the original timber joists. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams to reinforce the entire second floor. Getting those beams up the freight elevator was a disaster. The elevator got stuck between floors for four hours with two of the steelworkers inside. One of them was claustrophobic and started having a panic attack, so the fire department had to come and pry the doors open with the jaws of life. The whole thing made the evening news. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know what a doctor would say. They see things differently than we do, I guess. I'm just so afraid they'll tell me I'm making it all up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Look, I know you keep asking me that, and I'm sorry I keep getting off track. I know I'm avoiding the question. It's just... the whole reason I'm here is because I can’t stop seeing this droop. This awful unevenness around my mouth and my jaw. It is in every single photo, every reflection, and even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so overwhelmingly obvious to me that I’ve actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. 

And in architecture, avoiding clients is basically career suicide. I was supposed to lead a major pitch last month for this boutique hotel renovation, but I just couldn't do it. My partner had to step in. Which was probably for the best anyway, because the smart-blinds in the client's conference room malfunctioned and got stuck wide open. The sun glare completely washed out our 3D renderings on the projector. The IT guy—this kid who insists on wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts in the middle of January—spent twenty minutes trying to hack into the building's central control hub just to lower the shades. The developers were just sitting there, tapping their expensive pens, looking at their watches. We ended up having to pass around physical foam-core boards like it was the nineteen-nineties. It was incredibly embarrassing, and one of the investors actually made a joke asking if our structural designs were as glitchy as our AV equipment... 

But, um, anyway. The point is, I didn't go to that pitch because I couldn't stomach the idea of standing at the front of the room, talking, knowing everyone was staring at my crooked face. So, I don't know for sure what a surgeon or a dentist would find. I really don't. I just know what I see, and I can't keep hiding in my office forever.
