From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi... well, I'm here because I just can't stop seeing this... this awful droop and unevenness around my mouth and my jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. Actually, I was looking at some site photos the other day—I'm an architect, and we're doing this massive renovation on a mid-century modern house downtown. It's a gorgeous property, it has these original floor-to-ceiling windows that are just an absolute nightmare to source glass for right now. The supply chain is completely wrecked. My contractor, Dave, he's been calling every supplier from here to Chicago trying to find the right thermal tint, because if you don't get the tint exactly right, the whole facade looks cheap, you know? \n\nAnyway... I was looking at the photos Dave sent of the framing, and I caught my reflection in the glass in the background, and it just ruined my whole week. It's all I can look at. Even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so glaringly obvious to me. It's gotten so bad that I've actually started avoiding my presentations and my clients altogether. I just can't sit in a room and let them stare at my face looking like this.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your mouth and jaw that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just my mouth and my jaw. Just that whole lower section of my face. Honestly, I used to love wearing those masks on the job sites a couple of years ago. I kept wearing mine way longer than anyone else did just so I could cover it up. Dave—my contractor, the one I was just talking about—he used to joke that I was being overly cautious about the drywall dust. \n\nBut honestly, drywall dust is an absolute nightmare to get out of your clothes. We were doing a level five smooth finish on this massive gallery wall for a client last month, and with a level five, you have to skim coat the entire surface so it's perfectly flat. If the natural light hits it at the wrong angle, you see every single tiny bump or shadow. It takes forever to get right. The clients are this young couple who just moved here from Silicon Valley, and they just could not understand why it was taking an extra week. They kept texting me at all hours—like, 11 PM on a Sunday—asking why the painters weren't in yet. I had to explain to them, you can't just slap primer on unfinished drywall if you're planning to hang high-end art, it just doesn't work that way... \n\nI'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, about my face. No, it's just my jaw and my mouth. I don't really think about the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your daily life or relationships due to your concerns about your mouth and jaw?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it's, um, it's ruining everything, honestly. I actually cancelled a major client presentation at the very last minute the other day because I just... I couldn't bear the thought of them staring at my jaw. Which is terrible, because it was for this incredible brutalist revival project we're pitching. We're using poured-in-place concrete for the interior walls, and let me tell you, concrete is just so unforgiving. If the wooden forms aren't perfectly sanded and aligned, you get these awful seam lines and textures that you just cannot hide. My foreman is usually a wizard with it, but the temperature dropped last week and curing concrete in the cold is a complete nightmare. You have to use these massive thermal blankets, and they're so heavy to lug around the site... \n\nWait, sorry. You were asking about my life. Right. \n\nUm... well, besides the presentations, I've also been missing all this award-related networking stuff because I just can't face being seen by anyone in the industry. It's too humiliating. So my project deadlines are starting to completely fall apart because I'm just hiding out. And my family... I've been skipping family gatherings, too. My sister just had a big barbecue for my niece's graduation, and they catered from that place on 5th street with the amazing brisket. They smoke it over hickory for like fourteen hours, the bark on it is just incredible. I used to go there every Tuesday for lunch before they got super popular and raised their prices. \n\nBut anyway, I just... I couldn't go to the party. I stayed home. I just can't let people look at me right now, not even my own family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and mouth could be fixed or improved in some way, or do you see them as inherently flawed?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I'm not sure if it can actually be fixed, or if the underlying structure is just... wrong. Like it's just built wrong. \n\nIt's funny, when you use the phrase \"inherently flawed,\" it makes me think of this structural steel we got delivered for a cantilevered deck last spring. If we had installed those, the whole deck would have eventually sheared right off the side of the hill. We had to send the entire load back to the fabricator in Ohio. If it looked exactly the way I wanted... I mean, I guess? But I'm not really sure. Sometimes you get exactly what you want and it doesn't actually fix the whole picture, you know? \n\nLike, we had this client last year, this couple buying a place in the historic district, and they wanted their kitchen remodel exactly to this very specific mid-century spec. They spent months sourcing these original teak cabinets from Denmark, cost an absolute fortune, completely blew the budget. But when my crew pulled up the old linoleum to prep for the new tile, the entire subfloor was completely rotted out. But you can't just install perfect, expensive teak cabinets on a rotting floor. The whole house would eventually smell like mildew. And getting the revised permits for historic districts takes forever anyway. The lady at the zoning office, Brenda, she scrutinizes every single application like she's guarding the crown jewels, and she makes you submit everything in triplicate...\n\nOh, God. I can't even picture what my life was like before I started obsessing over this. I'd like to think it would fix everything, but I really just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about your jaw and mouth if you were completely alone and never saw yourself in reflections or photos?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess if I couldn't see myself at all, maybe I wouldn't think about it as much? But I feel like I'd still just *know* it was there. It's like this weight on me. \n\nAlthough, speaking of being completely alone without seeing anything... it reminds me of this site survey I had to do last winter up in the mountains. We were looking at this plot for an off-grid retreat center, and I got separated from the surveyor when this sudden whiteout blizzard hit. I was stuck in my truck for six hours, completely alone, couldn't see two feet past the windshield. The snow was piling up so fast I thought I was going to be buried alive. The heater in that old Ford F-250 barely works on a good day, and the blower motor was making this horrible screeching sound. I had to keep turning it off so it wouldn't burn out, and I was freezing. I tried calling my husband, but there's absolutely no cell service up there past the county line. The county actually voted down the new cell tower proposal because the local bird-watching society claimed it would disrupt the migration pattern of some rare owl. So I just sat there, shivering, eating stale granola bars I found in the glovebox...\n\nSorry. I'm drifting again. Um, if I was alone and couldn't see my face... I don't know. I think the feeling of it being wrong would still just be there, even in the dark.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be worried about what other people think of you if it weren't for your concerns about your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: Would I worry about what people think otherwise? I... I'm not really sure. I don't think so? I mean, you always have to care a little bit about what people think in my line of work, right? \n\nLike, we had this public consultation last month for a new mixed-use development by the riverfront, and I was so stressed about the community feedback. We spent weeks rendering these beautiful pedestrian walkways, and this one guy, this retired accountant or something, just monopolized the entire microphone for twenty minutes complaining about the brick color. We chose a classic Chicago common brick to match the historic warehouses next door, but he insisted it looked like dried ketchup. Dried ketchup! And then he started going off about the parking ratio, which isn't even my department, that's civil engineering. But the lead civil engineer, Dave, was out on paternity leave because his wife just had twins—a boy and a girl, actually, which is sweet, but Dave left his project files an absolute mess. I couldn't find the traffic impact study anywhere on the firm's shared drive. I spent three hours clicking through folders named things like \"Final_Final_v2\" just trying to find the parking metrics so I could answer this guy...\n\nOh, wow. I am so sorry. I keep doing this today, don't I? My mind just races. \n\nWhat I mean is... I really don't know if I'd be worried about other things. It's hard to even imagine not worrying about my mouth and my jaw. It just takes up all the space in my head, so I honestly don't know what else I'd be worrying about if it was gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your jaw and mouth are more about how you feel about them yourself, or about what you think others might think of them?\n\nPatient: I don't know. It's just so obvious. \n\nWhich is kind of like this situation we had with a load-bearing wall on a residential job last spring. The clients wanted this completely open-concept first floor, right? Just one massive space. But they bought this old Victorian, and you can't just knock down a central wall without putting in a massive steel I-beam. I told them it would drop the ceiling height by at least eight inches in the middle of the room, and it would be really obvious. They said they didn't care, they just wanted the open space. So we order the beam, get the crane out there, and the crane operator—this guy named Gary who always wears these incredibly bright neon yellow shirts, like, aggressively bright—he accidentally swings the beam too wide and clips the neighbor's oak tree. The neighbor was this retired judge who threatened to sue the entire firm over a broken branch. We had to get our legal department involved, and it turned into this whole mediation thing that dragged on for weeks. And after all that, when we finally got the beam installed and boxed it in with drywall, the clients hated it. They said it looked like a giant scar across the ceiling. I told them it was going to be obvious, but they just couldn't visualize it until it was right there, ruining the flow of the room...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... Like, I had this intern last summer, sweet kid from the state college, but he had absolutely no spatial awareness. If you opened the bathroom door, it would literally hit the espresso machine. But he just kept staring at his CAD software like the computer was going to fix it for him. Kids these days rely so much on the software, they don't even know how to hand-draft anymore. When I was in school, we spent the entire first year just doing hand-lettering and orthographic projections. My drafting professor, Mr. Henderson, he used to walk around with this red pen and if your line weight was even slightly off, he'd draw a giant X across your entire vellum sheet. It was brutal, but you learned to see things exactly as they were. You couldn't hide a mistake.\n\nUm... sorry. What I mean is, I'm not just imagining it. I see the mistake. It's right there on my face, and it's real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your jaw and mouth might look normal to others, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Have I considered that? I mean, people do tell me I look fine. My husband says it, my sister says it. But... I just can't see it that way. I'm here because I can't stop seeing this droop and unevenness around my mouth and jaw. It's in every single photo, every reflection. It feels so glaringly obvious that I've actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nLike, last month, I completely backed out of pitching this boutique hotel renovation downtown. I made my junior partner do it instead. Which was a massive risk, because the hotel owners are these notoriously difficult brothers who made their money in wholesale plumbing supplies. They were obsessed with the bathroom fixtures, obviously. They wanted these imported Italian rainfall showerheads that require a completely different water pressure system than what the century-old building could handle. I tried to explain in our early emails that the plumbing retrofits would cost a fortune, but they wouldn't listen. We had to bring in a specialized contractor, this guy named Sal who drives a van with a giant cartoon wrench painted on the side. Sal took one look at the existing copper pipes and just started laughing. It took three weeks just to repipe the third floor, and the drywall dust got absolutely everywhere. He claimed the herringbone pattern was too complicated for the grade of the yard, which was complete nonsense. We ended up having to eat the cost of the ruined bricks just to keep the clients happy, and they were already stressed because their golden retriever had just had surgery and they couldn't let the dog out back...\n\nSorry. I just... I don't know if a surgeon could fix it. I don't trust that they'd understand how bad it is, and what if I go through all that and it still looks wrong? Or worse? I mean, I think it's how they actually appear. It has to be. I look in the mirror and the asymmetry is just a physical fact. We just needed standard PVC for the drainage, and he kept trying to convince the client that they needed this specialized, acoustic-dampening cast iron pipe because the building was near a train track. The client was this tech startup, all 20-somethings who didn't know anything about construction, so they almost fell for it. I had to pull the CEO aside and explain that the train only ran twice a day and it was a freight line, so the acoustic pipes were a complete waste of their venture capital. And then the plumber got so offended he threatened to walk off the job, right in the middle of rough-in. I just don't know. You keep asking me, and I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. I mean, I see it, plain as day, but a professional? Maybe they would, maybe they wouldn't. \n\nIt reminds me of this structural engineer we work with sometimes, Dave. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams to reinforce the entire second floor. Getting those beams up the freight elevator was a disaster. The elevator got stuck between floors for four hours with two of the steelworkers inside. One of them was claustrophobic and started having a panic attack, so the fire department had to come and pry the doors open with the jaws of life. The whole thing made the evening news. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know what a doctor would say. They see things differently than we do, I guess. I'm just so afraid they'll tell me I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation would find any physical issue with your jaw and mouth that matches what you see?\n\nPatient: Look, I know you keep asking me that, and I'm sorry I keep getting off track. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I don't know. I really don't. You keep asking, and I wish I had a better answer for you, but I just don't know what a doctor would say. I mean, I'd like to think they'd see it, right? Because to me, it's just... it's right there.

We used these recycled PET felt panels on a library project last spring, and they completely transformed the space. The library was in this historic building with these gorgeous, vaulted ceilings, but the echo was so bad you couldn't even whisper. The head librarian was this very stern woman who kept shushing people, but it was the building's fault, not the patrons. We had to bring in a specialized acoustic engineer who ended up recommending these custom baffles that looked like floating clouds. The installation was a nightmare, though, because the scaffolding scratched the original 1920s hardwood floors, and the contractor tried to blame it on our team...

I'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I don't know if a plastic surgeon or a dentist would see the droop. I really don't. I'm just so scared they'll look at me and tell me I'm crazy.

We had this plumber on a commercial build last spring—a really nice guy, actually, brought his own espresso machine to the site every day—but he was obsessed with upselling. We just needed standard PVC for the drainage, and he kept trying to convince the client that they needed this specialized, acoustic-dampening cast iron pipe because the building was near a train track. The client was this tech startup, all 20-somethings who didn't know anything about construction, so they almost fell for it. I had to pull the CEO aside and explain that the train only ran twice a day and it was a freight line, so the acoustic pipes were a complete waste of their venture capital. And then the plumber got so offended he threatened to walk off the job, right in the middle of rough-in. We had to bribe him with premium parking just to get him to finish the bathrooms...\n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. It's just... I really don't know what a surgeon would say. I'm terrified they wouldn't see the droop, or that they'd just dismiss me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation, such as from a plastic surgeon or a dentist, would confirm that there is a physical issue with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I... I honestly don't know. A dentist, maybe? I haven't been to one in a while because I just can't stand the thought of them staring right at my mouth under those bright lights. \n\nSpeaking of those lights, we actually designed a pediatric dental clinic last year, and sourcing those specific overhead examination fixtures is an absolute nightmare. The head dentist wanted the whole place to be space-themed, so he ordered this massive custom fiberglass rocket ship for the waiting room. But the fabricator completely messed up the dimensions, and it wouldn't fit through the front double doors. We literally had to take out the entire front window pane just to get the thing inside. It was the middle of November, freezing cold, wind just howling through the lobby. The poor receptionist was huddled under her desk with a tiny space heater that kept tripping the circuit breaker because the electrical rough-in wasn't even fully signed off yet. And then the glass guys charged us double for an emergency weekend replacement, which completely blew the contingency budget... \n\nUm, anyway. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just... I don't know if a professional would confirm it. I'd like to think they would see the asymmetry because it's so obvious to me, but I'm just too terrified to actually go and find out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation from a plastic surgeon or a dentist would find any physical issue with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I really... I just don't know. You keep asking me, and I honestly couldn't tell you for sure. I mean, I see it, plain as day, but a professional? Maybe they would, maybe they wouldn't. \n\nIt reminds me of this structural engineer we work with sometimes, Dave. You can point out a crack in a foundation that looks like the whole building is about to slide down a hill, and he'll just tap it with his pen, look at his clipboard, and tell you it's just \"normal settling.\" But then you show him a tiny hairline fracture in a load-bearing column that you can barely even see without a magnifying glass, and he'll shut down the whole site. We had a project over in the warehouse district—this beautiful old brick building we were converting into lofts—and Dave found this microscopic issue with the original timber joists. We had to bring in these massive steel I-beams to reinforce the entire second floor. Getting those beams up the freight elevator was a disaster. The elevator got stuck between floors for four hours with two of the steelworkers inside. One of them was claustrophobic and started having a panic attack, so the fire department had to come and pry the doors open with the jaws of life. The whole thing made the evening news. \n\nSo... yeah. I don't know what a doctor would say. They see things differently than we do, I guess. I'm just so afraid they'll tell me I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a professional evaluation would find any physical issue with your jaw and mouth that matches what you see?\n\nPatient: Look, I know you keep asking me that, and I'm sorry I keep getting off track. I know I'm avoiding the question. It's just... the whole reason I'm here is because I can’t stop seeing this droop. This awful unevenness around my mouth and my jaw. It is in every single photo, every reflection, and even when people tell me I look fine, it feels so overwhelmingly obvious to me that I’ve actually started avoiding presentations and clients altogether. \n\nAnd in architecture, avoiding clients is basically career suicide. I was supposed to lead a major pitch last month for this boutique hotel renovation, but I just couldn't do it. My partner had to step in. Which was probably for the best anyway, because the smart-blinds in the client's conference room malfunctioned and got stuck wide open. The sun glare completely washed out our 3D renderings on the projector. The IT guy—this kid who insists on wearing these loud Hawaiian shirts in the middle of January—spent twenty minutes trying to hack into the building's central control hub just to lower the shades. The developers were just sitting there, tapping their expensive pens, looking at their watches. We ended up having to pass around physical foam-core boards like it was the nineteen-nineties. It was incredibly embarrassing, and one of the investors actually made a joke asking if our structural designs were as glitchy as our AV equipment... \n\nBut, um, anyway. The point is, I didn't go to that pitch because I couldn't stomach the idea of standing at the front of the room, talking, knowing everyone was staring at my crooked face. So, I don't know for sure what a surgeon or a dentist would find. I really don't. I just know what I see, and I can't keep hiding in my office forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe a professional evaluation by a plastic surgeon or dentist would find any physical issue with your jaw and mouth?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I really don't. You keep asking, and I wish I had a better answer for you, but I just don't know what a doctor would say. I mean, I'd like to think they'd see it, right? Because to me, it's just... it's right there. \n\nBut doctors are so unpredictable. Like my general practitioner, Dr. Evans. I went to him a few years ago because I had this persistent cough, and he told me it was just allergies. But the waiting room in his office—I couldn't even focus on what he was saying because the acoustics were a complete disaster. They had these hard linoleum floors and bare drywall, so every time the receptionist typed on her keyboard, it echoed down the hallway like a machine gun. I actually sketched out a whole proposal on a napkin while I was waiting, showing how they could install some acoustic baffle panels on the ceiling. It wouldn't even be that expensive. We used these recycled PET felt panels on a library project last spring, and they completely transformed the space. The library was in this historic building with these gorgeous, vaulted ceilings, but the echo was so bad you couldn't even whisper. The head librarian was this very stern woman who kept shushing people, but it was the building's fault, not the patrons. We had to bring in a specialized acoustic engineer who ended up recommending these custom baffles that looked like floating clouds. The installation was a nightmare, though, because the scaffolding scratched the original 1920s hardwood floors, and the contractor tried to blame it on our team...\n\nI'm sorry. I'm doing it again. I just... I don't know if a plastic surgeon or a dentist would see the droop. I really don't. I'm just so scared they'll look at me and tell me I'm crazy."
